The integral (log) cotangent complex of extensions of valued fields
Michaël Maex

Abstract
Let ( L , v L ) / ( K , v K ) (L,v_{L})/(K,v_{K}) be a finite or purely transcendental extension of real valued fields. We construct the associated integral cotangent and log cotangent complexes in terms of a MacLane-Vaquié chain approximating v L v_{L} . This leads to explicit formulas for associated invariants such as the (absolute) (log) different, weight norm and Kähler norm. As a corollary of our methods we obtain strong control of the higher homology of the integral (log) cotangent complex, generalizing an important result of Gabber and Ramero to the logarithmic setting.

1
Introduction

1.1
The integral (log) differentials and cotangent complex

Let
(L,v_{L})/(K,v_{K})
be an extension of valued fields, and let
L^{\circ},K^{\circ}
be their respective rings of integers.
The integral differentials,
\Omega_{L^{\circ}/K^{\circ}}
and the integral log differentials
\Omega^{\mathrm{log}}_{L^{\circ}/K^{\circ}}
are the source of many invariants in birational, analytic, and arithmetic geometry.
We highlight a few examples.

•

If
L/K
is an algebraic extension, then the different is the length of
\Omega_{L^{\circ}/K^{\circ}}
. The log different is defined analogously. These are important invariants in valuation and ramification theory. For example, see
[
18
, Chapitre III]
.

•

On Berkovich analytic spaces, an appropriate notion of log discrepancy defines weight functions that encode both birational information and arithmetic information, see
[
14
]
, and
[
7
]
.
The Kähler norm on
\Omega_{L/K}
is the unique norm such that
(\Omega_{L^{\circ}/K^{\circ}})_{tf}
is an almost unit ball
[
20
]
.

•

In the theory of embedded resolutions of a singular map
f:X\to\spec\mathbb{C}[T]
, the log discrepancy at each component
E
of a resolution with generic point
\eta
is the order of vanishing of the Gelfand-Leray form in the module
\Omega_{\mathcal{O}_{\eta}/\mathbb{C}[T]_{(T)}}^{\text{log,}\wedge\dim X-1}

[
13
, Lemma 9.6]
.
One of the most important invariants, the log canonical threshold, is the minimal value of the normalized log discrepancies at each component.

All of these invariants can be defined in terms of the length of specific torsion subquotients of the
L^{\circ}
-modules
\Omega_{L^{\circ}/K^{\circ}}
or
\Omega^{\mathrm{log}}_{L^{\circ}/K^{\circ}}
, and they can all be computed from a good description of these modules.
When
L
is not discretely valued (i.e.
L^{\circ}
is non-Noetherian), the length of an
L^{\circ}
-module is no longer a useful invariant.
Instead, we will use Temkin’s
content

[
20
, Section 2.6]
, which generalizes length and in particular, agrees with length when the value group of
L
is
\mathbb{Z}
.

While the original goal was to obtain a description of the module of integral (log) differentials that is sufficient to compute these invariants, it is actually easier to keep track of the whole (log) cotangent complex throughout the computation.
The cotangent complex of a ring morphism
A\to B
, denoted
\mathbb{L}_{B/A}
, is a derived version of the differentials
\Omega_{B/A}
and was first introduced in
[
6
]
.
The log cotangent complex, denoted
\mathbb{L}^{\mathrm{log}}_{B/A}
is an analogous version for log rings.
In this paper, the log cotangent complex will always refer to Gabber’s log cotangent complex as described in
[
17
, Section 8]
.

1.2
Motivation

This paper is part of the author’s PhD thesis, which aims to define and study weight functions on Berkovich spaces for the purpose of studying skeleta.
However, this construction of
\mathbb{L}_{L^{\circ}/K^{\circ}}
and
\mathbb{L}^{\mathrm{log}}_{L^{\circ}/K^{\circ}}
and computation of the associated invariants is an entirely value-theoretic matter, and is interesting in its own right due to the aforementioned connections to number theory and birational geometry.
For this reason, we publish this separately.
Except for a few remarks, we defer the applications in Berkovich geometry to a follow-up paper.

1.3
Main results

After the technical computation of the (log) cotangent complex of
L^{\circ}/K^{\circ}
when
L/K
is finite or purely transcendental (PT) in
??
we conclude with the main results in Sections
4
and
5
.
These consist of simple formulas for various invariants associated with extensions of valued fields in terms of augmentation chains, as well as control of the higher homology of the integral (log) cotangent complex.
By the nature of this kind of work, the main results are multiple smaller results. As not all invariants are defined for all extensions, it would be infeasible to repeat every result in the introduction.
Instead, we have assembled an overview of the main results in
??
.

1.4
The approach

Let
(K,v_{K})
be a non-trivially valued field.
One of the main technical tools we use are chains of ordinary and limit augmentations as introduced by M. Vaquié
[
21
,
23
,
22
]
and summarized by E. Nart in
[
12
]
.
In short, these give an explicit description of any semi-valuation,
w
, on
K[T]
extending
v_{K}
in terms of the data called key polynomials.
For each intermediate step of the chain, we define a ring
R_{i}
such that the union of these rings gives
(K^{\circ}[T]_{\ker w},w)^{\circ}
after a localization.
We can then keep track of the complexes
\mathbb{L}_{R_{i}/K^{\circ}}
and
\mathbb{L}^{\mathrm{log}}_{R_{i}/K^{\circ}}
, as well as the content of certain subquotients which define the aforementioned invariants.
The process is analogous to breaking up a complicated field extension into a series of smaller ones.
However, the rings
R_{i}
are not valuation rings.
Instead, we introduce the notion of
scalable
rings, which for these purposes behave similarly.
This way we can understand any finite and purely transcendental extension.

MacLane-Vaquié chains were chosen primarily because, unlike similar constructions, they work without any further assumptions on
K
.
The theory is “blind” to any base change behavior, which, for this work, is a feature as it avoids any wild ramification complications.
There are some further advantages.
The data in the chain is closely related to the metric and piecewise linear structure on the valuative tree. This will be studied more extensively in the special case of the Berkovich affine line in a follow up work.
The explicit nature makes it easy to construct and study examples.
The key polynomials match well to the structure of the log differentials, allowing us to directly compute the log differentials, whereas previous works have usually studied the log case by comparing it to the (non-log) differentials.
In fact
\{\dlog\varphi_{i}\}_{i\in I}
where
\varphi_{i}
runs over the key polynomials in an augmentation chain generate
\Omega^{\mathrm{log}}_{(K^{\circ}[T]_{\ker w})^{\circ}/K^{\circ}}
, see
??
, and each augmentation corresponds to an extension of the log differentials by a cyclic module.

1.5
Overview of the paper

??
is dedicated to valuation theory.
We start by fixing some conventions for (semi) valuations on rings and modules.
Then we give a brief overview of the theory of MacLane-Vaquié.
Finally, we introduce some notions that will be useful for the computations later on.

Most of the technical computations are contained in
??
.
Here we compute the (log) cotangent complex and associated invariants of an extension in terms of a MacLane-Vaquié chain.
Readers who are not interested in the methods can safely skip this section.

Sections
4
and
5
contain the main results of this paper.
The results are, for the most part, direct consequences of the computations in
??
.

1.6
Further questions

It is natural to ask whether the same computations can be carried out when we remove the assumption that valuations are real valued.
As the original motivation was Berkovich geometry, where only real valued fields are considered, we did not study this in detail.
Still, we can make the following conjectural remarks.
It is likely that a version of the computation in
??
still holds for more general valuations, although one will probably need to perform a more careful analysis of the cases (
\aleph
)-(
\daleth
) in
??
and add a case when
\mu
increases the rank of the value group.
With these modifications, we expect that all results regarding the structure of
\mathbb{L}_{(K[T]_{\ker w},w)^{\circ}/K^{\circ}}
and
\mathbb{L}^{\mathrm{log}}_{(K[T]_{\ker w},w)^{\circ}/K^{\circ}}
will generalize.
However, any results that concern the content of modules, suffer from the fact that no good notion of content exists for general modules over general valuation rings, see
[
4
, § 5.1.10-5.1.11]
.

1.7
Relation to other works

The idea that the integral log differential can contain interesting information about reductions of analytic spaces goes back to
[
8
]
and was further developed into the theory of weight functions in
[
10
,
1
]
.
This is what inspired the study of the log-discrepancy in this work.
In fact, the log-discrepancy can be extended to the weight valuation, see
??
.
Another metrization of differential forms on Berkovich spaces, the Kähler norm/valuation, appears in
[
20
]
.
We study this in
??
.

As far as the author is aware, the integral (log) differentials of
L/K
outside the case where
L
is discretely valued were first studied in
[
3
]
, and this has inspired further exploration, for example
[
20
]
and this paper.

Other works have related similar invariants to MacLane-Vaquié chains.
In
[
11
]
the different of a finite extension is computed from a MacLane chain in the discretely valued case.
In
[
15
]
the structure of
\Omega_{L^{\circ}/K^{\circ}}
is described in the special case when
L
is a pure extension of
K
.

Towards the end of the writing of this paper, the author was made aware of an unreleased draft by Katharina Hübner and Michael Temkin
[
4
]
, which obtains some overlapping results, albeit by different methods.
More precisely,
[
4
, Theorem 5.4.1]
is the same as
??
.
And,
[
4
, Lemma 2.5.3]
is a special case of
??
, when the MacLane-Vaquié chain consists of a single augmentation of degree
p
.

2
Valuations and augmentations

The purpose of this section is to fix some conventions and notation relating to valuations as well as introduce the theory of Vaquié-MacLane, which serves as our main tool for studying finite and purely transcendental extensions of valued fields.

??
introduces basic concepts on (semi-)valuations,
??
gives an overview of constructing semi-valuations on
K[T]
.
Finally, we use
??
to introduce a few concepts related to augmentation chains that will be useful in the computations later on.

2.1
Conventions on valuations

2.1.1 Definition
.

A semi-valuation on a ring
A
is a function
v:A\to(-\infty,\infty]
such that for all
a,b\in A

•

v(0)=\infty
,

•

v(a\cdot b)=v(a)+v(b)
,

•

v(a+b)\geq\min\{v(a),v(b)\}
.

A
semi-valued ring
is a pair
(A,v)
.
We further define

•

the
value group

\Gamma_{v}
to be the groupification of
v(A)\setminus\{\infty\}
. If the semi-valuation
v
on
A
is clear from context we may write
\Gamma_{A}
instead of
\Gamma_{v}
.
We write
\Gamma_{v}(\infty)=\Gamma_{v}\cup\{\infty\}
.

•

We say that
v
is
trivial
if
\Gamma_{v}=\{0\}
.

•

For any
r\in\mathbb{R}\cup\{-\infty,\infty\}
we write
A^{\geq r}=v^{-1}([r,\infty))
and
A^{>r}=v^{-1}((r,\infty))
.

•

The
kernel of

v
is
\ker v:=v^{-1}(\infty)
.

•

We say that
v
is a valuation if
\ker v=\{0\}
.

•

The
unit ball

A^{\circ}=A^{\geq 0}
and the
residue ring

\widetilde{A}=A^{\circ}/A^{>0}
.

2.1.2

The definition of a (semi-)valuation is not entirely standard across the literature.
Notably, we require that
\Gamma_{v}\subset\mathbb{R}
, i.e. our semi-valuations are of rank 1.
We require
\ker v=0
in the definition of a valuation, as is standard in the literature on non-archimedean geometry and number theory.
Most literature on augmentations and key polynomials such as
[
9
,
12
]
omit the requirement
\ker v=0
, and thus their valuation is what we call a semi-valuation.

2.1.3 Definition
.

Let
(A,v)
be a semi-valued ring and
M
an
A
-module.
Then a semi-valuation on
M
is a map
w:M\to\mathbb{R}(\infty)
such that for any
a,b\in M
and
c\in A

•

w(a+b)\geq\min\{w(a),w(b)\}
and,

•

w(c\cdot a)=v(c)+w(a)
.

Suppose that
B
is a
(A,v)
-algebra, then a semi-valuation
w
on
B
is a function that is both a semi-valuation when considering
B
as a ring and as an
A
-module, or equivalently it is a semi-valuation on the ring
B
that extends the semi-valuation on
A
.

2.2
A brief overview of MacLane-Vaquié theory

2.2.1

Let
(K,v_{K})
be a non-trivially valued field. Throughout the rest of the paper we will assume that all semi-valuations on
K[T]
are semi-valuations as a
K
-algebra, i.e. they extend the valuation of
K
.
Several efforts have been made to classify or describe the set of all semi-valuations on
K[T]
.
When
K
is discretely valued Saunders MacLane found a way of constructing all semi-valuations on
K[T]
in an iterative way
[
12
]
.
When
K
is complete, the set of semi-valuations on
K[T]
can be interpreted as the points of the Berkovich affine line.
In the special case when
K
is also algebraically closed Vladimir Berkovich classified all semi-valuations on
K[T]
in terms of decreasing chains of closed disks on
K
and used this to divide the points into types I-IV
[
2
, § 1.4.4]
.

Later work by Michelle Vaquié extends the approach of MacLane to remove any assumptions on
(K,v_{K})
other than
v
being non trivially valued
[
21
,
23
,
22
]
.
A nice overview paper on this was written by Enric Nart
[
12
]
, which serves as the main source for this subsection.
We only study rank 1 semi-valuations on
K[T]
. This simplifies some results in
[
12
]
, as we can uniformly choose the value group to a subgroup of
\mathbb{R}
everywhere.

2.2.2

The theory describes three ways of constructing a new semi-valuation on
K
-algebra
K[T]
from one or more semi-valuations on
K[T]
:
ordinary augmentations
,
limit augmentations
and
stable limits
, whose definitions we will recall shortly.
The main result of the theory,
??
, states that a semi-valuation
w
on
K[T]
can be obtained by starting from a Gauss semi-valuation
v_{0}
and the aforementioned building blocks.
By imposing simple restrictions on how we build
w
with these augmentations - known as a MacLane-Vaquié chain - one can even construct
w
in a canonical way up to some well described equivalence.

2.2.3 Definition
.

Let
\varphi\in K[T]
be a non-constant polynomial, and
f\in K[T]
be any polynomial.
Then the
\varphi
-expansion of
f
is the unique way of writing

f=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}\varphi^{i},

with
a_{i}\in K[T]
and
\deg(a_{i})<\deg(\varphi)
.
The
\varphi
-expansion can be easily computed using Euclid’s algorithm.

2.2.4 Definition
.

A
Gauss semi-valuation
on
K[T]
is a semi-valuation of the form

w:K[T]\to\mathbb{R}(\infty):\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}(T-\alpha)^{i}\mapsto\min_{i=0}^{n}\left\{v_{K}(a_{i})+i\cdot\mu\right\},

for a fixed
\alpha\in K
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}(\infty)
.
We say that
\alpha
is a
center of
w
and
\mu
is the
log-radius
.
To be consistent with notation later on, we will use the notation

w=[v_{K},T-\alpha,\mu].

Note that in our additive language the log-radius is simply the negative logarithm of the radius in the multiplicative language. For example a disk of radius
1
would have log-radius
0
.

2.2.5

Let
v
be a semi-valuation on
K[T]
and let
f,g,h\in K[T]
.
Then we say that

•

f
and
g
are
v
-equivalent
, written
f\sim_{v}g
, if
f=g=0
or
v(f-g)>v(f)
, equivalently
v(f-g)>v(g)
,

•

f
is
v
-divisible
by
g
, written
g\mid_{v}f
, if there is a
c\in K[T]
such that
gc\sim_{v}f
,

•

f
is
v
-irreducible
1
1

1

Perhaps
v
-prime would be a more apt name, but historically this is called
v
-irreducible.
if whenever
f\mid_{v}h_{1}h_{2}
with
h_{1},h_{2}\in K[T]
, then
f\mid_{v}h_{1}
or
f\mid_{v}h_{2}
,

•

f
is
v
-minimal if whenever
f\mid_{v}g
and
g\neq 0
, then
\deg f\leq\deg g
,

2.2.6 Definition
.

Let
v
be a semi-valuation on
K[T]
and
f\in K[T]
.
We say that
f
is a
key polynomial for
v
if
f
is monic,
v
-minimal, and
v
-irreducible.

Perhaps, the most important property of key polynomials is that they make the semi-valuation in the following definition well-defined.

2.2.7 Definition
.

An
ordinary augmentation
of degree
m
is a triple
\mathfrak{a}=(v,\varphi,\mu)
with
v
a semi-valuation on
K[T]
,
\varphi
a key polynomial for
v
with
\deg\varphi=m
, and
\mu\geq v(\varphi)
.

Then we define

[\mathfrak{a}]:K[T]\to\mathbb{R}:f=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}\varphi^{i}\mapsto\min_{i=0}^{n}\{v(a_{i})+i\cdot\mu\},

with
f=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}\varphi^{i}
the
\varphi
-expansion of
f
.
Then
[\mathfrak{a}]
is a semi-valuation with
[\mathfrak{a}]\geq v
.
We may also write this as
[v,\mathfrak{a}]
or
[v,\varphi,\mu]
.

2.2.8

In works cited on aumentations of semi-valuations the augmented semi-valuations are implicitly identified with the data defining the augmentation.
But we find it clearer to distinguish the two.
For us the augmentation will always refer to the defining data, and the resulting semi-valuation is referred to as the augmented semi-valuation.

2.2.9 Definition
.

Let
I
be a totally ordered set and
(v_{i})_{i\in I}
a sequence of semi-valuations on a ring
A
.
Then we say that
(v_{i})_{i\in I}
is a
stable family
if
(v_{i}(a))_{i\in I}
stabilizes for every
a\in A
, and we say that
\lim_{i}v_{i}
is the
stable limit
of
(v_{i})_{i\in I}
.

2.2.10 Definition
.

A
continuous family
of degree
m
and log-radius
\mu
is a tuple
\mathfrak{a}=(v,(\psi_{i},\gamma_{i})_{i\in I})
where

•

I
is a totally ordered set without maximal element,

•

each
(v,\psi_{i},\gamma_{i})
is an ordinary augmentation of degree
m
.
For the remainder of the definition we write
v_{i}=[v,\psi_{i},\gamma_{i}]
.

•

For each
i<j\in I
,
(v_{i},\psi_{j},\gamma_{j})
is an ordinary augmentation, with
\psi_{j}\not\sim_{v_{i}}\psi_{i}
.

Moreover, we say that
\mathfrak{a}
is

•

stable
if
(v_{i})_{i\in I}
is a stable family,

•

almost stable
if it is not stable and
\lim_{i}\gamma_{i}=\infty
,

•

essential
if is not stable and
(v_{i}(f))_{i\in I}
stabilizes for every
f\in K[T]
with
\deg(f)\leq m
.

We define
[\mathfrak{a}]=\lim_{i}v_{i}
to be the pointwise limit, which also is a semi-valuation.

2.2.11 Definition
.

Continue with the notation as in
??
.
If
\mathfrak{a}
is essential, a
limit key polynomial for
\mathfrak{a}
is a monic
f\in K[T]
of minimal degree with the property that
(v_{i}(f))_{i\in I}
does not stabilize.

2.2.12 Remark
.

Continue with the context of the previous definition.
By the line before Lemma 3.4 in
[
12
]
we see that
(v(\psi_{i}))_{i\in I}
is constant and for
i<j\in I
, it holds that
[v,\psi_{i},\gamma_{i}]=[v,\psi_{j},\gamma_{i}]
.
So we see that
(\gamma_{i})_{i\in I}
is strictly increasing.
Intuitively, the family of key polynomials
(\psi_{i})
give increasingly accurate directions in the valuative tree, and the
\gamma_{i}
’s tell us how far in the valuative tree the augmentation moves.

2.2.13

Note that if
\mathfrak{b}
is almost stable and
f\in K[T]
such that
(v_{i}(f))_{i\in I}
does not stabilize, then necessarily
\lim_{i}v_{i}(f)=\infty
.
Indeed, let
f=\sum_{j=0}^{n}a_{j}\psi_{i}^{j}
be the
\psi_{i}
expansion and write
v(\psi)
for the constant value of
v(\psi_{i})
.
The function

p_{i}:[v(\psi),\gamma_{i}]\to\mathbb{R}\infty:r\mapsto[v,\psi_{i},r](f)=\min_{i=0}^{n}\{v(a_{j})+jr\},

is the minimum of non-decreasing affine functions, and as such non-decreasing with decreasing slopes.
If
p_{i}
would stabilize at some point then there would be
r_{1}<r_{2}\in[v(\psi_{i}),\gamma_{i}]
and
v_{r_{1}}:=[v,\psi_{i},r_{1}],v_{r_{2}}:=[v,\psi_{i},r_{2}]
, and
v\leq v_{r_{1}}<v_{r_{2}}\leq v_{i}
such that
v_{r_{1}}(f)=v_{r_{2}}(f)
.
But this contradicts that
(v_{i}(f))_{i\in I}
does not stabilize, see
[
12
, Corollary 2.5(ii)]
.
Thus
p_{i}
has at least slope 1 at every point and thus
v_{i}(f)\geq v(f)+(\gamma_{i}-v(\psi))
, which tends to
\infty
as
\gamma_{i}
tends to
\infty
.

2.2.14 Definition
.

A
limit augmentation
of degree
m
and log-radius
\mu
is a tuple
\mathfrak{a}=(v,(\psi_{i},\gamma_{i})_{i\in I},\varphi,\mu)
such that
\mathfrak{b}=(v,(\psi_{i},\gamma_{i})_{i\in I})
is an essential continuous family,
\varphi
is a limit key polynomial for
\mathfrak{b}
,
\deg\varphi=m
, and
\mu\geq[v,\psi_{i},\gamma_{i}](\varphi)
for all
i\in I
.
Sometimes we may write
(\mathfrak{b},\varphi,\mu)
instead.

We define

[\mathfrak{a}]:K[T]\to\mathbb{R}:f=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}\varphi^{i}\mapsto\min_{i=0}^{n}\{[\mathfrak{b}](a_{i})+i\cdot\mu\},

with
f=\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}\varphi^{i}
the
\varphi
-expansion of
f
.
This defines a semi-valuation, see
[
12
, Proposition 3.5]
, which we may sometimes denote as
[\mathfrak{b},\varphi,\mu]
.
Moreover, we say that
\mathfrak{a}
is
almost stable
if
\mathfrak{b}
is almost stable.

Note that if a limit augmentation
\mathfrak{a}=(v,(\psi_{i},\gamma_{i})_{i\in I},\varphi,\mu)
is almost stable, then necessarily
\mu=\infty
by
??
.

2.2.15 Definition
.

An
augmentation

\mathfrak{a}
on
v
is an ordinary augmentation, limit augmentation, or a stable continuous family on
v
.
Recall that in each case we denote the augmented semi-valuation by
[\mathfrak{a}]
.

2.2.16 Definition
.

Let
v
be a semi-valuation on
K[T]
.
An
augmentation chain
on
v
is a sequence of augmentation data
(\mathfrak{a}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
with
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\cup\{\infty\}
,
\mathfrak{a}_{1}
an augmentation on
v
and for all
i<n
,

•

\mathfrak{a}_{i}
is an ordinary or limit augmentation,

•

\mathfrak{a}_{i+1}
is an augmentation on
[\mathfrak{a}_{i}]
.

In particular, a stable continuous family may only appear at the end of the chain.

Let
w
be a semi-valuation on
K[T]
.
We say that an augmentation chain
(\mathfrak{a}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}

approximates

w
if
w=[\mathfrak{a}_{n}]
when
n\neq\infty
or
w
is the stable limit of
([\mathfrak{a}_{i}])_{i}
when
n=\infty
.
We write this as
w=[(\mathfrak{a}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}]
.

2.2.17 Notation
.

Let
w,v
be two semi-valuations on
K[T]
. Then we write

K[T]_{w=v}=\{f\in K[T]\mid v(f)=w(f)\},\quad K[T]_{w\neq v}=\{f\in K[T]\mid v(f)\neq w(f)\},

and let
\Phi_{v,w}
be the subset of monic polynomials of minimal degree in
K[T]_{v\neq w}
.

If
w\geq v
one should think of
\Phi_{v,w}
as the set of key polynomials on
v
that point towards
w
in the valuative tree.

2.2.18 Definition
.

An augmentation chain
(\mathfrak{a}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
on
v_{0}
is a
MacLane-Vaquié chain
if it consists of only ordinary and limit augmentations and for every
i
with
1\leq i<n

•

when
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
is ordinary, then
\deg\varphi_{i-1}<\deg(\Phi_{v_{i-1},v_{i}})
,

•

when
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
is limit, then
\deg\varphi_{i}=\deg(\Phi_{v_{i-1},v_{i}})
and
\varphi_{i-1}\not\in\Phi_{v_{i-1},v_{i}}
,

where
v_{j}=[\mathfrak{a}_{j}]
,
\varphi_{j}
is the key polynomial of
\mathfrak{a}_{j}
and
\varphi_{0}
is a key polynomial for
v_{0}
of minimal degree.

2.2.19 Theorem

(The main theorem of Vaquié,
[
12
, Thm 4.3]
)
.

Any semi-valuation
w
on
K[T]
falls into exactly one of the following cases, denoted (a), (b), and (c) following loc. cit.

(a)

There is a Gauss semi-valuation
v
and a finite MacLane-Vaquié chain
(\mathfrak{a}_{i})_{i}
on
v
such that
(\mathfrak{a}_{i})_{i}
approximates
w
.

(b)

There is a Gauss valuation
v
and a finite augmentation chain
(\mathfrak{a}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
on
v
approximating
w
such that
(\mathfrak{a}_{i})^{n-1}_{i=1}
is a MacLane-Vaquié chain and
\mathfrak{a}_{n}
is a stable continuous family augmentation with
\deg v_{n-1}=\deg\Phi_{v_{n-1},w}
and
\varphi_{n-1}\notin\Phi_{v_{n-1},w}
, with
\varphi_{n-1}
the key polynomial of
\mathfrak{a}_{n-1}
.

(c)

There is a Gauss valuation
v
and an infinite MacLane-Vaquié chain
(\mathfrak{a}_{i})_{i}
on
v
such that
(\mathfrak{a}_{i})_{i}
approximates
w
.

2.2.20

These chains are unique up to some well described equivalence. Its precise formulation is a bit involved, and we will not need it going further.
The interested reader may consult
[
12
, Section 4.3]
.
Some remarks on how
??
relates to other classifications of semi-valuations on
K[T]
are in order.

2.2.21 Remark
.

If
K
is discretely valued, the theorem relates to MacLane’s original classification
[
9
, Thm 8.1]
as follows.
This classification constructs any semi-valuation as the last semi-valuation in a finite chain of ordinary augmentations or a point-wise (not necessarily stable) limit of an infinite chain of ordinary augmentations, called an inductive semi-valuation and a limit semi-valuation respectively.

Suppose that
w
is an inductive semi-valuation, then clearly
w
falls in case (a) and all
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
are ordinary augmentations.
Suppose that
w
is a limit semi-valuation, and
([v_{i-1},\varphi_{i},\mu_{i}])_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
the augmentation chain of ordinary augmentations.

•

If
(\deg\varphi_{i})_{i}
is unbounded, then
\lim_{i}v_{i}
is necessarily a stable limit. Thus,
w
belongs to case (c).

•

If
(\deg\varphi_{i})_{i}
is bounded and stabilizes at
i=n
then we may put all augmentations of top degree
(\mathfrak{a}_{i})_{i=n}^{\infty}
in a continuous family
\mathfrak{b}
.

–

If
\mathfrak{b}
is unstable, then
w
belongs to case (b) by discreteness.

–

If
\mathfrak{b}
is inessential, then we may replace it by one ordinary augmentation and
w
belongs to case (a).

–

If
\mathfrak{b}
is essential, we may choose a limit key polynomial
\psi
and then the chain
(\mathfrak{a}_{0},\ldots,\mathfrak{a}_{n-1},(\mathfrak{b},\psi,\infty))
is a chain approximating
w
.
Thus,
w
belongs to case (a).

Note that when
K
is discretely valued, the only limit augmentation that may appear in a chain from
??
is an almost stable limit augmentation at the end of the chain.
Note that if
K
is complete, this cannot occur.

2.2.22 Remark
.

If
K
is complete and algebraically closed, then all key polynomials are linear.
As a result, all MacLane-Vaquié chains are of length
0
on a Gauss semi-valuation
v(x-a)=\mu
which is the supremum norm on the disk with center
a
and log-radius
\mu
.
So case (c) does not occur. In case (a), the semi-valuation is simply the supremum norm on a disk.
And in case (b) it is a limit of the supremum norms on a chain of decreasing disks.
Thus, the theorem reduces to the classical Berkovich classification theorem.

2.2.23 Remark
.

If
K
is complete, the semi-valuations on
K[T]
are points in the Berkovich affine line
\mathbb{A}_{K}^{\text{an}}
.
There is a correspondence between the cases of
??
and the type I, II, III, IV points on
\mathbb{A}^{1,\text{an}}_{K}
.
Let
v
be a semi-valuation on
K[T]
and
(\mathfrak{a}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
be an augmentation chain approximating
v
as in
??
.
Let
x
be the corresponding point of
\mathbb{A}^{\text{an}}_{K}
.
If
v
falls in case (a), then
x
is of type I, II, III if
\mu=\infty
,
\mu\in\Gamma_{v}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
,
\mu\not\in\Gamma_{v}\otimes\mathbb{Q}\cup\{\infty\}
respectively with
\mu
the log-radius of
\mathfrak{a}_{n}
.
If
v
falls in case (b), then
x
is of type IV.
Finally, if
v
falls in case (c), then
x
is of type IV or a non-rigid type I point, which means that
\mathcal{H}(x)
is not a finite field extension of
K
.
The author does not know of a simple criterion to distinguish between type IV and I in this case.

2.3
Steps and
w
-optimal augmentations

2.3.1

In this section, we introduce the concepts of the step of an augmentation and
w
-optimal augmentations, which will be useful for the results and computations in the following sections.

2.3.2 Definition
.

Let
w
be a semi-valuation on
K[T]
and let
\mathfrak{a}
be an augmentation on
v
.
We say that
\mathfrak{a}
is
w
-optimal
if

•

\mathfrak{a}=(v,\varphi,\mu)
is an ordinary augmentation, and
\mu=w(\varphi)
,

•

or
\mathfrak{a}=(v,(\psi_{i},\gamma_{i})_{i\in I})
is a continuous family, and each ordinary augmentation
(v,\psi_{i},\gamma_{i})
is
w
-optimal,

•

or
\mathfrak{a}=(v,(\psi_{i},\gamma_{i})_{i\in I},\varphi,\mu)
is a limit augmentation, and for each
i
we have
\gamma_{i}=w(\psi_{i})
and
\mu=w(\varphi)
.

We say that an augmentation chain is
w
-optimal if each augmentation in the chain is
w
-optimal.

2.3.3

By
[
12
, Corollary 2.5.(2)]
we see that a chain
(\mathfrak{a}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
approximating
w^{\prime}
with
w^{\prime}\leq w
is
w
-optimal if and only if for every
i<n
we have that
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
is
[\mathfrak{a}_{i}]_{i+1}
-optimal and, if
n
is finite,
\mathfrak{a}_{n}
is
w
-optimal.

2.3.4 Proposition
.

Let
w
be any semi-valuation on
K[T]
, and let
(\mathfrak{a}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
be an augmentation chain approximating
w
as in
??
.
Then
(\mathfrak{a}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
is
w
-optimal.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{a}_{i}
be an ordinary or limit augmentation with (limit) key polynomial
\varphi_{i}
and
\mu_{i}=[\mathfrak{a}_{i}](\varphi_{i})
.
If it is the last augmentation in the chain then
w=[\mathfrak{a}_{i}]
and clearly
\mu_{i}=w(\varphi_{i})
.
Else, consider the next augmentation
\mathfrak{a}_{i+1}
.
If it is an ordinary or limit augmentation then it’s part of the MLV-chain, thus
\varphi_{i}\not\in\Phi_{[\mathfrak{a}_{i}],[\mathfrak{a}_{i+1}]}
.
So by
[
12
, Corollary 2.5]
we see that
\varphi_{i}\not\in\Phi_{[\mathfrak{a}_{i}],w}
.
On the other hand, if
\mathfrak{a}_{i+1}
is a continuous family, then we are in case (b) of
??
, and then also
\varphi_{i}\not\in\Phi_{[\mathfrak{a}_{i}],w}
.
In all cases we can conclude that
w(\varphi_{i})=\mu_{i}
.
So we have already shown that all ordinary augmentations are
w
-optimal and the second half of the definition for limit augmentations.
It remains to show that the continuous families (and those in the limit augmentations) are
w
-optimal.
Let
\mathfrak{b}=(v^{\prime},(\psi_{i},\gamma_{i})_{i\in I})
be such a continuous family.
Take any
i\in I
and choose a
j>i
, which exists as
I
has no maximal element.
By the properties written between 3.3 and 3.4 in
[
12
]
we see that
[v^{\prime},(\psi_{j},\gamma_{j})](\psi_{i})=\gamma_{i}
.
Thus, by
[
12
, Corollary 2.5]
we see that
\gamma_{i}=w(\psi_{i})
.
∎

2.3.5

The
w
-optimal condition ensures that all the individual steps in the augmentation process are controlled in a way suitable for the computations in
??
.
We would also like our chains to start with as simple a semi-valuation as possible, like the Gauss valuation with
v=[v_{K},T,0]
.
Then we have the nice property that
(K[T],v)^{\circ}=K^{\circ}[T]
.
As this is not always possible, we slightly relax this to the notion of a
simple
Gauss valuation which is defined by
w=[v_{K},x-a,\mu]
with
\mu\in\Gamma_{K}
.
Let
S=b^{-1}(x-a)
. Then
(K[T],w)^{\circ}=(K[S],w)^{\circ}=K^{\circ}[S]
.

2.3.6 Corollary
.

Any semi-valuation
w
on
K[T]
can be approximated by a chain of
w
-optimal augmentations on a simple Gauss valuation.

Proof.

By
??
,
??
there exists a
w
-optimal chain approximating
w
on a Gauss semi-valuation
v_{1}=[v_{K},x-a,\mu_{1}]
.
Choose any
r\in\Gamma_{K}
with
r<\mu_{1}
and let
v_{0}
be the simple Gauss valuation defined by
v_{0}(x-a)=r
.
We can now prefix the chain with the ordinary augmentation
\mathfrak{a}_{0}=(v_{0},x-a,\mu_{1})
, to obtain a
w
-optimal chain on
v_{0}
.
∎

2.3.7 Definition
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}
be an augmentation on
v
. We define the
step of

\mathfrak{a}
as follows.

•

If
\mathfrak{a}=(v,\varphi,\mu)
is an ordinary augmentation, then

\step\mathfrak{a}=\mu-v(\varphi)

•

If
\mathfrak{a}=(v,(\psi_{i},\gamma_{i})_{i\in I})
is a continuous family, then

\step\mathfrak{a}:=\lim_{i\in I}(\gamma_{i}-v(\psi_{i})).

•

If
\mathfrak{a}=(v,(\psi_{i},\gamma_{i})_{i\in I},\varphi,\mu)
is a limit augmentation with
\mathfrak{b}=(v,(\psi_{i},\gamma_{i})_{i\in I})=(\mathfrak{b}_{i})
the continuous family.
Then

\step\mathfrak{a}:=\step\mathfrak{b}+\lim_{i\in I}(\mu-[v,\psi_{i},\gamma_{i}](\varphi)).

If
(\mathfrak{a}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
is an augmentation chain then we define

\step\left((\mathfrak{a}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\step\mathfrak{a}_{i}.

2.3.8

Note that for a
\mathfrak{a}=(v,(\psi_{i},\gamma_{i})_{i\in I})
the sequence
(\gamma_{i}-v(\psi_{i}))
is increasing by
??
.

2.3.9

Step measures how far in the valuative tree the augmentation has moved the valuation.
Actually, the step of a chain on
v
approximating
w
depends only on
v
and
w
.
We won’t prove this here as it will be an immediate corollary of
??
.

3
The (log) cotangent complex associated to augmentations

Throughout this section we take
(K,v_{K})
to be a non-trivially valued field, with no further assumptions.

3.0.1

Let
w
be a semi-valuation on the polynomial ring
K[T]
.
The goal of this section is to construct the cotangent complex, and its logarithmic analogue associated to the extensions
(K[T]_{\ker w},w)^{\circ}/K^{\circ}
and
(K[T]_{\ker w},w)^{\circ}/K^{\circ}[\varphi_{0}]
for some appropriate linear polynomial
\varphi_{0}
, using the data in a
w
-optimal augmentation chain approximating
w
.
The main idea is to use the augmentation chain to construct the ring
(K[T]_{\ker w},w)^{\circ}
from
K^{\circ}[\varphi_{0}]
using locally complete intersections, directed increasing unions and localizations.
These are all operations under which the behavior of (log) differentials is well understood.

3.1
Notation and helpful definitions

3.1.1
Measuring torsion with content

When
K
is discretely valued and
M
is a torsion
K^{\circ}
-module, then classically the “size of
M
” is measured by
\length M
.
One of the main goals of this section is to understand the torsion part of
\Omega_{\mathcal{H}^{\circ}(x)/K^{\circ}}
.
However, the definition of
\length
only makes sense when
K^{\circ}
is Noetherian, i.e. when
K
is discretely valued.

In
[
20
, § 2.6]
, the
content
of a
K^{\circ}
-module is defined, which generalizes
\length
, even when
K^{\circ}
is non-Noetherian.
Content, like the rest of
[
20
]
, is phrased in the multiplicative language of norms, whereas we use the additive language of valuations.
For the convenience of the reader, we will rephrase the definition, and repeat and expand some basic properties in the additive language.

3.1.2 Definition
.

Let
M
be a finitely presented
K^{\circ}
-module.
There is an isomorphism
M\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\frac{K^{\circ}}{(\alpha_{i})}
for some
\alpha_{i}\in K^{\circ}
.
Then we define
\cont(M)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{K}(\alpha_{i})
.
Note that some
\alpha_{i}
may be zero, in which case
\cont(M)=\infty
.
If
M
is not finitely presented, we define

\cont(M)=\sup_{i}\cont(M_{i}),

where
M_{i}
runs through all finitely presented subquotients of
M
.

If
\Gamma_{K}=\gamma\cdot\mathbb{Z},\gamma>0
and
M
is finitely presented, then
\cont M=\gamma\cdot\length M
.
In particular, when
\gamma=1
then
\length
and content agree, but content has the further advantage of rescaling automatically when
\gamma\neq 1
.
Like
\length
, content is also additive under exact sequences.

3.1.3 Lemma
.

Let
\ldots\xrightarrow{f_{-2}}M_{-1}\xrightarrow{f_{-1}}M_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{0}}M_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}\ldots
be an exact sequence of
K^{\circ}
-modules.
Then

\sum_{i\text{ even}}\cont(M_{i})=\sum_{i\text{ odd}}\cont(M_{i}).

Proof.

For every
i
we consider the SES

0\to\im f_{i-1}\to M_{i}\to\im f_{i}\to 0.

Then by
[
20
, Theorem 2.6.7]
we find
\cont(M_{i})=\cont(\im f_{i-1})+\cont(\im f_{i})
.
Thus,

\sum_{i\text{ even}}\cont(M_{i})=\sum_{i}\cont(\im f_{i})=\sum_{i\text{ odd}}\cont(M_{i}).

∎

3.1.4 Lemma
.

Suppose that a
K^{\circ}
-module
M
is a filtered union of submodules
M_{i}
. Then,

\cont(M)=\lim_{i}\cont(M_{i}).

Proof.

This is
[
20
, Lemma 2.2.6]
.
∎

3.1.5

Content does not commute with arbitrary filtered colimits
(M_{i})_{i\in I}
when it is not an increasing union, i.e. the maps
M_{i}\to M_{j}
are not /injections. For example, let
M
be any module with non-zero content and take the colimit of
(M)_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
where all the maps are zero.
However, if the kernels of the maps
M_{i}\to M_{j}
tend to “almost vanish” when
i
increases, then content still commutes. We make this precise in the following lemma.

3.1.6 Lemma
.

Let
(M_{i})_{i\in I}
be a filtered system of
K^{\circ}
-modules with colimit
M
and write
f_{ij}:M_{i}\to M_{j}
,
f_{i}:M_{i}\to M
for the natural maps.
Then,

\cont(M)=\lim_{i}\cont(M_{i}),

if one of the following conditions holds

(a)

\lim_{i}\cont\left(\ker f_{i}\right)=0

(b)

\lim_{i}\lim_{j\geq i}\cont\left(\ker f_{ij}\right)=0

(c)

(\cont M_{i})_{i\in I}
converges and
\lim_{i}\lim_{j\geq i}\cont\left(\coker f_{ij}\right)=0
.

Proof.

We first show
(a)
.
By replacing
I
by a cofinal subsystem, we may assume that
\cont(\ker M_{i})
is finite for all
i
.
For any
i\in I
there is a SES

0\to\ker f_{i}\to M_{i}\to\im f_{i}\to 0.

Note that
M=\colim_{i}\im f_{i}
is an increasing union.
Hence,
??
,
??
shows that

\cont(M)=\lim_{i}\cont(\im f_{i})=\lim_{i}\cont(M_{i})-\cont(\ker f_{i})=\lim_{i}\cont(M_{i}).

We can deduce (b) from (a) by observing that for a fixed
i
,
(\ker f_{ij})_{j\geq i}
is an increasing union resulting in
\ker f_{i}
.
We find that
\lim_{j\geq i}(\cont(\ker f_{ij}))=\cont(\ker f_{i})
from
??
.

Now we focus our attention on (c).
By passing to a cofinal subsystem of
I
we assume that
\cont(M_{i})<\infty
for all
i
.
Then there is an exact sequence

0\to\ker f_{ij}\to M_{i}\to M_{j}\to\coker f_{ij}\to 0.

Because the kernel and cokernel are subquotients of modules of finite content, they themselves have finite content.
By
??
, we see that

\lim_{i}\lim_{j\geq i}\cont(\ker f_{ij})=\left(\lim_{i}\lim_{j\geq i}\cont M_{i}-\cont M_{j}\right)+\left(\lim_{i}\lim_{j\geq i}\cont(\coker f_{ij})\right).

Thus, we now deduce condition (b) from the fact that the sequence
(\cont M_{i})_{i\in I}
is Cauchy and the condition on the cokernels.
∎

The following result is used without proof in
[
20
]
, for example in the proof of
[
20
, Theorem 5.2.11]
.
For the sake of completeness, we provide a full proof.

3.1.7 Lemma
.

Let
(L,v_{L})/(K,v_{K})
be an extension of valued fields and let
M
be a
K^{\circ}
-module.
Then

\displaystyle\cont_{L^{\circ}}(M\otimes_{K^{\circ}}L^{\circ})=\cont_{K^{\circ}}(M).

Proof.

Suppose that
M
is finitely presented.
Then we can find an isomorphism
M\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\frac{K^{\circ}}{(\alpha_{i})}
and then clearly
M\otimes_{K^{\circ}}L^{\circ}\simeq\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\frac{L^{\circ}}{(\alpha_{i})}
.
Thus,

\cont_{L^{\circ}}(M\otimes_{K^{\circ}}L^{\circ})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}v(\alpha_{i})=\cont_{K^{\circ}}(M).

Now suppose that
M
is finitely generated.
If
K^{\circ}
is Noetherian, then
M
is also finitely presented, so the result follows from the previous case.
Now suppose that
K^{\circ}
is not Noetherian, i.e.
\Gamma_{K}
is dense in
\mathbb{R}
.
There is some surjection
(K^{\circ})^{n}\to M
with kernel
A
.

0\to A\to(K^{\circ})^{n}\to M\to 0.

If
A
is not a semilattice of
K^{n}
, i.e.
\dim_{K}A\otimes_{K^{\circ}}K<n
, then
\cont(M)=\infty
.
Similarly,
A\otimes_{K^{\circ}}L^{\circ}
is not a semilattice in
L^{n}
as

\dim_{L}(A\otimes_{K^{\circ}}L^{\circ})\otimes_{L^{\circ}}L=\dim_{L}(A\otimes_{K^{\circ}}K)\otimes_{K}L<n.

So
\cont_{L^{\circ}}(M\otimes_{K^{\circ}}L^{\circ})=\infty=\cont_{K^{\circ}}(M)
.
Likewise, we see that if
A
is a semilattice in
K^{n}
, then it is a semilattice in
L^{n}
.
Then we see that
[(K^{\circ})^{n}:A]=[(L^{\circ})^{n}:A\otimes_{K^{\circ}}L^{\circ}]
, and we conclude that
\cont_{L^{\circ}}(M\otimes_{K^{\circ}}L^{\circ})=\cont_{K^{\circ}}(M)
from
[
20
, Lemma 2.6.4]
.
Here
[\cdot,\cdot]
denotes the
index of lattices
as described in
[
20
, § 2.5.9]
.

Finally, let
M
be any
K^{\circ}
-module.
Then
M
is a filtered union of finitely generated submodules
M_{i}
.
By
[
19
,
Tag 00DD
]
we see that
M\otimes_{K^{\circ}}L^{\circ}
is also a filtered union of
(M_{i}\otimes_{K^{\circ}}L^{\circ})_{i}
as
L^{\circ}
is flat over
K^{\circ}

[
3
, Remark 6.1.12(ii)]
.
So we may conclude from
??
that
\cont(M\otimes_{K^{\circ}}L^{\circ})=\cont(M)
.
∎

3.1.8
Constants and scalable rings

3.1.9

In an effort to make the computations in this section more clear, it will be useful to introduce some new notation and definition, in particular enlargements, constants, and scalable rings.
These are quite natural notions, but to the best of the author’s knowledge not defined elsewhere.

An
enlargement
models an ordinary augmentation on the level of unit balls of rings.
Roughly speaking, an enlargement adds elements to the unit ball, which corresponds to increasing the valuation of these elements.
We also introduce the notions of
constant
elements and
scalable
rings, which can be thought of as an orthogonality condition between the value group and the residue ring. This ensures that we can always rescale an element of the ring to have any valuation of the value group.
This is necessary to ensure that some finitely presented rings, that approximate enlargements, are cut out by complete intersections.

Throughout this subsection, let
(A,v)
be a semi-valued
K
-algebra with value group
\Gamma
.

3.1.10 Definition
.

Let
(B,w)
be a non-trivially semi-valued ring.
An element
a\in B
is a
constant
if
a\notin\ker w
and for every
b\in B
, it holds that

w(a)\leq w(b)\implies a\mid b.

When
w(a)\geq 0
, this is equivalent to the statement that
B^{\geq w(a)}
is generated by
a
as a
B^{\circ}
module.
The set of constants is denoted by
\vd B
.
We say that
B
is
scalable
if for every
r\in w(B)\setminus\{\infty\}
, there exists a constant
a
with
w(a)=r
, i.e.,
w(\const B)=w(B)\setminus\{\infty\}
.

In a scalable ring, any element can be “rescaled” to an element of any valuation by multiplying or dividing by by a constant with an appropriate value.

3.1.11 Example
.

Let
(K,v_{K})
be a discretely valued field with
\Gamma_{K}=\mathbb{Z}
, and consider the Gauss valuation
w=[v_{K},T,1/2]
.
Then
T
is not a constant, nor is there any other constant with valuation
1/2
.
Hence,
(K[T],w)
is not scalable.
However,
(K[T,T^{-1}],w)^{\circ}
is scalable.

The notion simplifies a bit when we assume that the rings are
K
-algebras.

3.1.12 Lemma
.

If
A
is a
K
-algebra, then

\vd(A)=A^{\times},\quad\vd(A^{\circ})=A^{\times}\cap A^{\circ}.

Proof.

The inclusions
A^{\times}\subset\vd(A)
, and
A^{\times}\cap A^{\circ}\subset\vd(A^{\circ})
are immediate.
Conversely, suppose that
a
is a constant in
A^{\circ}
or
A
.
Then there is some
b\in K^{\times}
with
v(b)\geq v(a)
, so
a\mid b
and thus
a\in A^{\times}
.
∎

3.1.13 Lemma
.

The
K
-algebra
A
is scalable if and only if
A^{\circ}
is scalable.

Proof.

\implies

Let
r\in\Gamma_{v,\geq 0}
.
Then there exists a constant
a\in A
with
v(a)=r
.
It is easy to see that
a
is also a constant as an element of
A^{\circ}
.

\impliedby

Let
r\in\Gamma_{v}
and let
b\in K
with
-r\leq v(b)<\infty
.
Then
v(b)+r\geq 0
, so there is a constant
a
of
A^{\circ}
with valuation
r+v(b)
.
Then
a
is a unit in
A
, and
b^{-1}a
is also a unit in
A
.
So
b^{-1}a
is a constant of valuation
r
.
∎

3.1.14

Let
w
be a semi-valuation on
A
with
w\geq v
.
We say that an element
a\in A
is
w
-stable
if
w(a)=v(a)
.
All constants of
A
and
A^{\circ}
are
w
-stable.
Indeed, let
a
be a constant, then
w(a)\geq v(a)
and
w(a^{-1})\geq v(a^{-1})
from which equality follows.

3.1.15 Lemma
.

Suppose that
A
is a scalable domain.
Let
S
be a multiplicative subset of
A
.
Then
(S^{-1}A)^{\circ}=T^{-1}A^{\circ}
, where
T=\{a^{-1}s\mid s\in S,a\in\vd(A),v(a)=v(s)\}
is a multiplicative set.
Moreover, this ring is also scalable.
In particular, the unit ball of a localization of
A
is a localization of
A^{\circ}
.

Proof.

Any element of
(S^{-1}A)^{\circ}
can be written as
\frac{a}{b}
with
a\in A,b\in S
and
v(a)\geq v(b)
.
Let
c
be a constant with
v(c)=v(b)
.
Then

\frac{a}{b}=\frac{a/c}{b/c},\quad a/c\in A^{\circ},b/c\in T

So
a/b\in T^{-1}A^{\circ}
.

Conversely, any element of
T^{-1}A^{\circ}
can be written as
\frac{a}{c^{-1}b}
, where
a\in A^{\circ}
,
b\in S
,
c\in\vd(A)
with
v(c)=v(b)
.
Then
ac\in A
and
b\in S
. So
\frac{a}{c^{-1}b}
is also contained in
(S^{-1}A)^{\circ}
.
∎

3.1.16
Enlargements

3.1.17 Definition
.

Let
(A,v)
be a scalable domain and
\varphi\in A
an irreducible element with
\mu\geq v(\varphi)
.
Then the
(\varphi,\mu)
-enlargement
of
A^{\circ}
is

[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]=A^{\circ}[a^{-1}\varphi^{n}\mid a\in\vd(A),n\in\mathbb{Z},n\cdot\mu-v(a)\geq 0]

3.1.18

These enlargements are useful because they help us understand how unit balls change under ordinary augmentations, as is indicated by the following lemma.

3.1.19 Lemma
.

Suppose that
\varphi
is an irreducible element of
A
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
with
\mu\geq v(\varphi)
.
Let
A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu
be as in
??
and let
w\geq v
be a semi-valuation with
w(\varphi)=\mu
.
Suppose that

v^{\prime}:A\to\mathbb{R}:a\mapsto\max\left\{\min_{i=0,\ldots,n}\{v(a_{i})+\mu\cdot i\}\nonscript\;\,|\,\nonscript\;\forall a_{0},\ldots,a_{n}\in A:\sum_{i=0}^{n}a_{i}\varphi^{i}=a\right\}

is well-defined and defines a semi-valuation on
A
.
Here, well-defined means that the maximum is achieved for each
a\in A
.
Then

[(A,v)^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]=\begin{cases}(A[\varphi^{-1}],v^{\prime})^{\circ}&\text{if }\mu\neq\infty\\
(A,v^{\prime})^{\circ}&\text{if }\mu=\infty\end{cases}.

Moreover, if
(A,v)^{\circ}
is scalable, then so is
([(A,v)^{\circ},\varphi,\mu],v^{\prime})
.

Proof.

We only consider the case when
\mu\neq\infty
. The case when
\mu=\infty
is similar.
Let
a\in\vd(A)
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
n\cdot\mu-v(a)\geq 0
.
Then
v^{\prime}(a^{-1}\varphi^{n})\geq n\cdot\mu-v(a)\geq 0
.
Thus,
a^{-1}\varphi^{n}\in(A[\varphi^{-1}],v^{\prime})^{\circ}
.
So we can conclude that
[(A,v)^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]\subset(A[\varphi^{-1}],v^{\prime})^{\circ}
.
Conversely, suppose that
a\in(A[\varphi^{-1}],v^{\prime})^{\circ}
.
Then for some
a_{n},\ldots,a_{m}\in A
, we can write
a=\sum_{i=n}^{m}a_{i}\varphi^{i}
with
v^{\prime}(a)\leq v(a_{i})+\mu\cdot i
for all
i=n,\ldots,m
.
So
v(a_{i})+\mu\cdot i\geq 0
.
Let
b_{i}\in A^{\circ}
be a constant with
v(b_{i})=v(a_{i})
.
Then we can write
a_{i}\varphi^{i}=(a_{i}/b_{i})b_{i}\varphi^{i}
with
a_{i}/b_{i}\in A^{\circ}
and
v(b_{i})+\mu\cdot i\geq 0
.
So
a\in[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]
.

Now suppose that
(A,v)^{\circ}
is scalable.
Note that
v\leq v^{\prime}\leq w
.
Clearly,
v^{\prime}([A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu])=\left<\Gamma_{v},\mu\right>_{\geq 0}
.
Let
r\in\left<\Gamma_{v},\mu\right>_{\geq 0}
, with
r\neq 0
.
Then
r
can be written as
r^{\prime}+i\cdot\mu
for some
r\in\Gamma_{v}
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Note that as
r<\infty
either
i=0
or
\mu<\infty
.
If
i=0
, then any constant
a
of
(A^{\circ},v)
is a unit in
A
.
So
a
is also a unit of
A
or
A[\varphi^{-1}]
, thus it is also a constant of the unit ball
([(A,v)^{\circ},\varphi,\mu],v^{\prime})
.
It is also a constant because it is a unit in
A[\varphi^{-1}]
.
So we conclude that
([(A,v)^{\circ},\varphi,\mu],v^{\prime})
is scalable.
∎

3.2
Explicit descriptions of enlargements and their cotangent complexes

3.2.1

In this section we will describe
enlargements
and their relative differentials/cotangent complexes.
This is a technical computation.
To aid the reader, we will first give an overview of the computation.
Suppose that
(A,v)
is a scalable domain with value group
\Gamma
.
Let
\varphi\in A
be a prime element, and let
\mu=w(\varphi)\geq v(\varphi)
.
We will need to consider four cases which we will refer to using the first four letters of the Hebrew alphabet,

(
\aleph
)

\mu\in\mathbb{Q}\otimes\Gamma_{v}
,

(
\beth
)

\mu\not\in\mathbb{Q}\otimes\Gamma_{v}(\infty)
and
\Gamma_{v}
is not discrete,

(
\gimel
)

\mu\not\in\mathbb{Q}\otimes\Gamma_{v}(\infty)
and
\Gamma_{v}
is discrete,

(
\daleth
)

\mu=\infty
.

Sometimes we may split up case (
\aleph
) into subcases depending on whether
\Gamma_{v}
is discrete or not, denoted by (
\aleph
.d) and (
\aleph
.i) respectively.

3.2.2

For each step in the computation, we will need to consider the four cases separately, using analogous but slightly different arguments.
Each case has its own subtleties that need to be addressed individually.
Unfortunately, the author does not know of a way to unify the arguments.
Some notation is specific to a single case and will be introduced throughout the computation.
This notation remains consistent across all lemmas, propositions, theorems, proofs, etc., and will not be reintroduced each time.

1.

In
??
we show that
[(A,v)^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]
can be written as an increasing union of rings
A_{i},i\in I
where
I
is a directed set and each
A_{i}
has an explicit finite presentation as an
A^{\circ}
-algebra.

2.

In
??
we show that the relations in the presentations of
A_{i}
obtained in
??
form a regular sequence. Thus, each
A_{i}
is a complete intersection over
A^{\circ}
.

3.

In
??
we use this to show that
\mathbb{L}_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}}\simeq\Omega_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}}[0]
and give an explicit finite presentation of the latter module.

4.

In
??
we show that the valuation of the determinants of the presentations found in
??
converge as
i
increases to
\mu-v(\varphi)
up to a fudge term - analogous to the step of an augmentation.
These valuations of the determinants are
\cont(\Omega_{A_{i}/A}\otimes_{A_{i}}L^{\circ})
for some suitable valued field
L
.

5.

In
??
, we take a colimit over
i\in I
to show that

\mathbb{L}_{[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]/A^{\circ}}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}L^{\circ}\simeq\left(\Omega_{[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]/A^{\circ}}\otimes L^{\circ}\right)[0]

and that the latter is a module of content
\mu-v(\varphi)
up to some fudge terms.
The main difficulty in this step is checking that content commutes with the colimit in this case.

6.

In
??
we interpret the results of
??
for augmentations on semi-valuations.

??
and
??
we compute the cotangent complex associated to an ordinary and limit augmentation.
In
??
we use distinguished triangles of cotangent complexes to understand the situation for augmentation chains.

7.

In Sections
3.4
and
3.5
we prove analogous versions of these statements for the log cotangent complex.
We even get a more explicit description of the log differentials than for the (non-log) differentials.

8.

Finally, in
??
and
??
we use the results of our computations to prove some results about the (log) cotangent complex, (log) differents and (log) discrepancies.

3.2.3 Notation
.

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
.
Then we define
\overline{(a,b)}
to be the minimal tuple
(c,d)\in(\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0})^{2}
such that

ac-bd=\gcd(a,b).

3.2.4 Proposition
.

Suppose that
(A,v)
is a scalable domain with value group
\Gamma
.
Let
\varphi\in A
be a prime element, and let
\mu=w(\varphi)\geq v(\varphi)
.
Let
\theta\in A
be a constant with
v(\theta)=v(\varphi)
.
Then
[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]
has the following explicit description as an increasing union of finitely presented
A^{\circ}
-algebras over a directed poset.

(
\aleph
)

Case
\mu\in\Gamma_{v,\mathbb{Q}}
:

Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
be the minimal element such that
\mu\cdot m\in\Gamma
.
Let
\alpha
be a constant with valuation
\mu\cdot m
.
Then
[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]
is an increasing union

[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]=\bigcup_{(\beta,\ell)\in I}A_{(\beta,\ell)},\quad A_{(\beta,\ell)}=A^{\circ}[\alpha^{-1}\varphi^{m},\alpha\varphi^{-m},\beta^{-1}\varphi^{\ell}].

(3.2.a)

Here

I=\left\{(\beta,\ell)\in A^{\times}\times\mathbb{Z}\nonscript\;\,|\,\nonscript\;\gcd(\ell,m)=1,\frac{e\mu m+v(\varphi)}{d}\leq v(\beta)<\ell\mu\text{ with }(d,e)=\overline{(\ell,m)}\right\}

is the filtered system where
(\gamma,k)\geq(\beta,\ell)
if and only if
q\cdot(-v(\gamma)+k\mu)\leq-v(\beta)+\ell\mu
, with
q
the smallest positive integer such that
q\cdot k\equiv\ell\mod m
.

Moreover, for each
i:=(\beta,\ell)\in I
, the ring
A_{i}
has a finite presentation as an
A^{\circ}
-algebra,

\displaystyle A_{i}\simeq\frac{A^{\circ}[X,X^{-1}][Y_{i}]}{(g_{i},h_{i})},

\displaystyle g_{i}=X^{-\ell}Y_{i}^{m}-\alpha^{\ell}\beta^{-m},\quad h_{i}=\alpha^{-e}\beta^{d}\theta^{-1}X^{-e}Y_{i}^{d}-\theta^{-1}\varphi

(3.2.b)

\displaystyle X\mapsto\alpha^{-1}\varphi^{m},\quad Y_{i}\mapsto\beta^{-1}\varphi^{\ell},

with
(d,e)=\overline{(\ell,m)}
.
Note that the conditions on
v(\beta)
in the definition of
I
ensure that
v(\alpha^{-e}\beta^{d}\theta^{-1})\geq 0
and
v(\alpha\beta^{-m})\geq 0
.

(
\beth
)

Case
\mu\not\in\Gamma_{v,\mathbb{Q}}(\infty)
and
\Gamma_{v}
is not discrete:

Then
[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]
is an increasing union

[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]=\bigcup_{(\alpha,\beta)\in I}A_{(\alpha,\beta)},\quad A_{(\alpha,\beta)}=A^{\circ}[\alpha^{-1}\varphi,\beta\varphi^{-1}],

where
I=\{(\alpha,\beta)\in A^{\times}\times A^{\times}\nonscript\;\,|\,\nonscript\;v(\varphi)\leq v(\alpha)\leq\mu\leq v(\beta)\}
is the directed set where
(\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime})\geq(\alpha,\beta)\iff v(\alpha^{\prime})\geq v(\alpha),v(\beta^{\prime})\leq v(\beta)
.

Moreover, for each
i=(\alpha,\beta)
, the ring
A^{\circ}[\alpha^{-1}\varphi,\beta\varphi^{-1}]
admits the following presentation,

\displaystyle\frac{A^{\circ}[X_{i},Y_{i}]}{(g_{i},h_{i})},\quad

\displaystyle g_{i}=X_{i}Y_{i}-\alpha^{-1}\beta,h_{i}=\theta^{-1}\alpha X_{i}-\theta^{-1}\varphi

\displaystyle X_{i}\mapsto\alpha^{-1}\varphi,Y_{i}\mapsto\beta\varphi^{-1}.

(
\gimel
)

Case
\mu\not\in\Gamma_{v,\mathbb{Q}}(\infty)
and
\Gamma_{v}
is discrete:

Suppose that
\Gamma_{v}=\gamma\cdot\mathbb{Z},\gamma>0
and let
\pi
be a constant with
v(\pi)=\gamma
.
Let
\frac{a_{0}}{b_{0}},\frac{a_{1}}{b_{1}},\frac{a_{2}}{b_{2}},\ldots
be the convergents (in reduced form) of the continued fraction expansion of
\gamma^{-1}(\mu-v(\varphi))
.

Then
[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]
is an increasing union

[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}A_{i},\quad A_{i}=A^{\circ}[\pi^{-a_{2i}}(\theta^{-1}\varphi)^{b_{2i}},\pi^{a_{2i+1}}(\theta^{-1}\varphi)^{-b_{2i+1}}].

Moreover, for each
i
, the ring
A_{i}
admits the following presentation,

\displaystyle\frac{A^{\circ}[X_{i},Y_{i}]}{(g_{i},h_{i})},\quad

\displaystyle g_{i}=X_{i}^{b_{2i+1}}Y_{i}^{b_{2i}}-\pi,h_{i}=X_{i}^{a_{2i+1}}Y_{i}^{a_{2i}}-\theta^{-1}\varphi

\displaystyle X_{i}\mapsto\pi^{-a_{2i}}(\theta^{-1}\varphi)^{b_{2i}},Y_{i}\mapsto\pi^{a_{2i+1}}(\theta^{-1}\varphi)^{-b_{2i+1}}.

(
\daleth
)

Case
\mu=\infty
:

Then
[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]
is an increasing union

\displaystyle[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]=\bigcup_{\alpha\in I}A^{\circ}[\alpha^{-1}\varphi],

where
I=\{\alpha\in A^{\times}\mid v(\alpha)>v(\varphi)\}
with
\beta\geq\alpha\iff v(\beta)\geq v(\alpha)
.
Moreover, for each
\alpha\in I
the ring
A^{\circ}[\alpha^{-1}\varphi]
admits the following finite presentation as an
A^{\circ}
-algebra,

\displaystyle\frac{A^{\circ}[X_{\alpha}]}{g_{\alpha}},\quad g_{\alpha}=\theta^{-1}\alpha X_{\alpha}-\theta^{-1}\varphi,\quad X_{\alpha}\mapsto\alpha^{-1}\varphi.

Before we proceed to the proof, a few remarks are in order.

3.2.5 Remark
.

Although the proposition involves several cases, they all stem from the same idea.
We can write

[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]=A^{\circ}[\alpha^{-1}\varphi^{n}\mid\alpha\in\vd(A),(-v(\alpha),n)\in C],

where
C=\{(a,b)\in\Gamma_{v}\times\mathbb{Z}\mid b\cdot\mu+a\geq 0\}
is a submonoid of
\Gamma_{v}\times\mathbb{Z}
.
The idea is to describe
[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]
as an increasing union of explicit
A^{\circ}
-algebras by approximating
C
with an increasing union of convenient submonoids.

3.2.6 Remark
.

The case (
\aleph
) simplifies in two different ways in the subcases (
\aleph
.d) and (
\aleph
.i)
In subcase (
\aleph
.d), i.e. when
\Gamma_{v}
is discrete, the filtered system
I
has a maximal element,
(\pi^{a},\ell)
, with
a,\ell\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that
\ell\mu-av(\pi)=v(\pi)/m
, where
\pi
is a constant with
v(\pi)
a positive generator of
\Gamma_{v}
.
So
[A^{\circ},\mu,\varphi]
is itself is a finitely presented
A^{\circ}
-algebra, and as we will later see in
??
even a complete intersection over
A^{\circ}
.

In subcase (
\aleph
.i), i.e. when
\Gamma
is not discrete, the limit simplifies in another way.
In this case
I^{\prime}=\{(\beta,\ell)\in I\mid\ell=1\}
is a cofinal subsequence of
I
, and we may instead consider the limit over
I^{\prime}
.
For
(\beta,1),(\gamma,1)\in I^{\prime}
we see that simply
(\gamma,1)\geq(\beta,1)\iff v(\gamma)\geq v(\beta)
.

3.2.7 Remark
.

Assume that
(K,v_{K})
is complete,
A=K[T]
,
v=[v_{K},T,0]
and
\varphi=T
.
If
\mu\not\in\Gamma_{v,\mathbb{Q}}(\infty)
then
w=[v,\varphi,\mu]
is a type III point in
\mathbb{A}_{K}^{1,\text{an}}
.
Then in both cases (regardless of whether
\Gamma
is discrete or not) the rings
A_{i}
are the intersections of
K[T,T^{-1}]
with the unit balls of affinoid algebras corresponding to annuli shrinking around the type III point
w
.
Intuitively, we can think of this as approximating
(K[T,T^{-1}],w)^{\circ}
with “affine models” of shrinking annuli around
w
.

Proof of
??
.

In all cases, it is clear that the indexing set, either
I
or
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, is a directed poset.
And in cases (
\aleph
), (
\beth
), and (
\daleth
), it is also clear that the union is increasing and equals
[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]
.

In case
(\gimel)
, i.e. when
\mu\not\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}}(\infty)
and
\Gamma
is discrete, this needs an argument.
We know from the classical theory of continued fractions that
a_{2i}b_{2i+1}-a_{2i+1}b_{2i}=-1
.
From the classical theory of finitely generated monoids, we know that
\left<(-a_{2i},b_{2i}),(a_{2i+1},-b_{2i+1})\right>
is a simplicial cone in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and thus precisely equal to the cone

C_{i}:=\left<(-a_{2i},b_{2i}),(a_{2i+1},-b_{2i+1})\right>=\left\{(a,b)\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}\nonscript\;\,|\,\nonscript\;b\cdot\frac{a_{2i}}{b_{2i}}-a\geq 0,\quad b\cdot\frac{a_{2i+1}}{b_{2i+1}}-a\geq 0\right\}.

Let
\psi=\theta^{-1}\varphi
. Then we see that

A_{i}=A^{\circ}[\pi^{-a_{2i}}\psi^{b_{i}},\pi^{a_{2i+1}}\psi^{-b_{2i+1}}]=A^{\circ}[\pi^{a}\psi^{b}\mid(a,b)\in C_{i}],

from which we see that the sequence
A_{i}
is increasing because the sequence
C_{i}
is increasing.

Next, consider
\alpha^{-1}\varphi^{n}
with
n\in\mathbb{Z},\alpha\in\vd(A)
and
n\mu-v(\alpha)\geq 0
.
The inequality is in fact strict because
\mu\not\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}}
.
Let
b=v(\alpha\theta^{-n})/v(\pi)
.
Then we can write
\beta\pi^{b}=\alpha\theta^{-n}
with
\beta
a constant with
v(\beta)=0
.
We may rewrite this as
\beta\pi^{b}\psi^{n}
with
n\gamma^{-1}(\mu-v(\varphi))-b>0
.
Because the continued fraction convergents
a_{i}/b_{i}
approximate
\gamma^{-1}(\mu-v(\varphi))
we know that for
i\gg 0
,
n\frac{a_{2i}}{b_{2i}}-b\geq 0
and
n\frac{a_{2i+1}}{b_{2i+1}}-b\geq 0
.
Thus,
(n,b)\in C_{i}
.
So, there are
\ell,k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
such that
(n,b)=\ell(a_{2i},b_{2i})+k(a_{2i+1},b_{2i+1})
.
Then we see that

(\pi^{-a_{2i}}(\theta^{-1}\varphi)^{b_{2i}})^{\ell}(\pi^{a_{2i+1}}(\theta^{-1}\varphi)^{-b_{2i+1}})^{k}=\pi^{b}(\theta^{-n}\psi^{n}),

from which we may conclude that
\alpha^{-1}\varphi^{n}=\beta\pi^{b}\psi^{n}\in A_{i}
.
This shows that the union of the rings
A_{i}
is indeed equal to
[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]
.

This leaves us to show that the given presentations for the
A_{i}
’s are correct in each case.

(
\aleph
)

Case
\mu\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}}
:

Fix an
i=(\beta,\ell)\in I
.
Let
f:\frac{A^{\circ}(X)[Y_{i}]}{(g_{i},h_{i})}\to A^{\circ}[\alpha^{-1}\varphi^{m},\alpha\varphi^{-m},\beta^{-1}\varphi^{\ell}]
be the induced morphism.
It is easy to see that
g_{i},h_{i}
vanish, thus
f
is well-defined and that
f(X)=\alpha^{-1}\varphi^{m},f(X^{-1})=\alpha\varphi^{-m},f(Y_{i})=\beta^{-1}\varphi^{\ell}
making
f
surjective.

We will show that
f
is also injective.
Let
x
be in the domain of
f
such that
f(x)=0
.
Let
x=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{x}}a_{j}X^{n_{j}}Y^{m_{j}}
be a representative of
x
with a minimal number of terms.
If any of the coefficients
a_{j}
are divisible by
\varphi
in
A
we may factor out the largest power of
\theta^{-1}\varphi
and use relation
h_{i}
to substitute
\theta^{-1}\varphi
with
\alpha^{-e}\beta^{d}\theta^{-1}X^{-e}Y^{d}_{i}
.
Thus, we may assume all
a_{j}
’s are indivisible by
\varphi
in
A
.
We further assume that all
m_{j}\leq m
as we can use the relation
g_{i}
to substitute
Y_{i}^{m}
for
\alpha\beta^{-m}X^{\ell}
.
Note that these substitutions do not change the number of terms, so we still have a representation with a minimal number of terms.

No two terms share the same monomial as we chose a representation with a minimal number of terms.
Because
\gcd(\ell,m)=1
and
0\leq m_{j}<m
we know that the
\varphi
-adic order of
f(a_{j}X^{n_{j}}Y^{m_{j}})

v_{\varphi}(f(a_{j}X^{n_{j}}Y_{i}^{m_{j}}))=n_{j}\cdot m+m_{j}\cdot\ell,

is different for each
j=1,\ldots,n_{x}
.
Thus,

v_{\varphi}(x)=\inf_{j=1,\ldots,n_{x}}\{n_{j}\cdot m+m_{j}\}=\infty,

which means that
n_{x}=0
, thus
x=0
.

The arguments for the other cases are analogous and can be treated in less detail.

(
\beth
)

Case
\mu\not\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}}
,
\mu\neq\infty
, and
\Gamma
is not discrete:

Fix an
i=(\alpha,\beta)\in I
and let
f:\frac{A^{\circ}[X_{i},Y_{i}]}{g_{i},h_{i}}\to A^{\circ}[\alpha^{-1}\varphi,\beta\varphi^{-1}]
be the induced morphism.
Then, like in the previous case, we see that
f
is well-defined and surjective.

To show injectivity, take an
x
in the domain of
f
with
f(x)=0
, and let
x=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{x}}a_{j}X_{i}^{n_{j}}Y_{i}^{m_{j}}
be a representative with a minimal number of terms.
Using the relation
h_{i}
we may assume that none of the coefficients
a_{j}
are divisible by
\varphi
.
Further, using relation
g_{i}
we may assume that all monomials are either powers of
X_{i}
or powers of
Y_{i}
, i.e. for every
j
either
n_{j}=0
or
m_{j}=0
.
As the representation has a minimum number of terms we know that no two terms share the same monomial.
So we see again that the
\varphi
-adic order of
f(a_{j}X_{i}^{n_{j}}Y_{i}^{m_{j}})=n_{j}-m_{j}
is different for each
j
.
Thus,

v_{\varphi}(x)=\inf_{j=1,\ldots,n_{x}}\{n_{j}-m_{j}\}=\infty.

Therefore,
n_{x}=0
, and hence
x=0
.

(
\gimel
)

Case
\mu\not\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}}
,
\mu\neq\infty
, and
\Gamma
is discrete:

Fix an
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and let
f:\frac{A^{\circ}[X_{i},Y_{i}]}{g_{i},h_{i}}\to A^{\circ}[\pi^{-a_{2i}}(\theta^{-1}\varphi)^{b_{2i}},\pi^{a_{2i+1}}(\theta^{-1}\varphi)^{-b_{2i+1}}]
be the induced morphism.
Like before, we see that
f
is well-defined and surjective, and we take
x
in the domain with
f(x)=0
.
Let
x=\sum_{j=1}^{n_{x}}a_{j}X_{i}^{n_{j}}Y_{i}^{m_{j}}
be a representative with a minimal number of terms.
Again using relation
h_{i}
we may assume that none of the coefficients
a_{j}
are divisible by
\varphi
.
Write
s_{j}=n_{j}b_{2i}-m_{j}b_{2i+1}
.
Then using relation
g_{i}
we may assume that each pair of exponents
(n_{j},m_{j})
is the unique minimal positive solution to the Diophantine equation
n_{j}b_{2i}-m_{j}b_{2i+1}=s_{j}
.
As we assumed that
n_{x}
is minimal we know that no two pairs
(n_{j},m_{j})
are the same and thus all
s_{j}
’s are distinct.
We see that
v_{\varphi}(f(a_{j}X_{i}^{n_{j}}Y_{i}^{m_{j}}))=s_{j}
and thus

v_{\varphi}(x)=\inf_{j=1,\ldots,n_{x}}\{s_{j}\}=\infty

from which we conclude that
n_{x}=0
, and hence
x=0
.

(
\daleth
)

Case
\mu=\infty
:

This is a much simpler version of the argument of the previous cases.

∎

3.2.8 Computation
.

Continue with the context of
??
.
Suppose that we are in case (
\aleph
), (
\beth
), or (
\gimel
), and let
A_{i}
be one of the rings with presentation
A^{\circ}[X_{i},Y_{i}]/(g_{i},h_{i})
or
A^{\circ}[X_{i}^{\pm 1},Y_{i}]/(g_{i},h_{i})
.
Then the ring
A^{\circ}[X_{i},Y_{i}]/(g_{i})
or
A^{\circ}[X_{i}^{\pm 1},Y_{i}]/(g_{i})
has a simple explicit form and in particular is a domain.

(
\aleph
)

Case
\mu\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}}
:

Let
i=(\beta,\ell)\in I
and let
B_{i}=A^{\circ}[\alpha^{-1}T^{m},\alpha T^{-m},\beta^{-1}T^{\ell}]
, where
T
is a free variable.
Then
f:A^{\circ}[X_{i}^{\pm 1},Y_{i}]/(g_{i})\to B_{i}
with
X_{i}\mapsto\alpha^{-1}T^{m},Y_{i}\mapsto\beta^{-1}T^{\ell}
is an isomorphism.

The proof strategy is the same as in the proof of
??
.
Clearly
f
is well-defined and surjective.
Let
x\in\ker f
and choose a representation
\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}X^{n_{j}}Y^{m_{j}}
with a minimal number of terms.
Then we may use relation
g_{i}
to assume that
m_{j}\leq m
for all
j=1,\ldots,n
by substituting
Y_{i}^{m}
for
\alpha\beta^{-m}X^{\ell}
.
Then all terms have different
T
-adic orders, and thus
n=0
and
x=0
.

(
\beth
)

Case
\mu\not\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}}(\infty)
, and
\Gamma
is not discrete:

Let
i=(\alpha,\beta)\in I
and let
B_{i}=A^{\circ}[\alpha^{-1}T,\beta T^{-1}]
.
Then
f:A^{\circ}[X_{i},Y_{i}]/(g_{i})\to B_{i}
with
X_{i}\mapsto\alpha^{-1}T,Y_{i}\mapsto\beta T^{-1}
is an isomorphism.
Again it is well-defined and surjective.
Let
x=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}X^{n_{j}}Y^{m_{j}}\in\ker f
be a minimal representative.
Using the relation
g_{i}
we may assume that for all
j
either
n_{j}=0
or
m_{j}=0
.
Hence, the
T
-adic order of each term differs and so
n=0
and
x=0
.

(
\gimel
)

Case
\mu\not\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}}(\infty)
and
\Gamma
is discrete:

Let
i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and let
B_{i}=A^{\circ}[\pi^{-a_{2i}}T^{b_{2i}},\pi^{a_{2i+1}}T^{-b_{2i+1}}]
.
Then
f:A^{\circ}[X_{i},Y_{i}]/(g_{i})\to B_{i}
with
X_{i}\mapsto\pi^{-a_{2i}}T^{b_{2i}},Y_{i}\pi^{a_{2i+1}}T^{-b_{2i+1}}
is an isomorphism.
Again it is well-defined and surjective.
Let
x=\sum_{j=1}^{n}a_{j}X^{n_{j}}Y^{m_{j}}\in\ker f
be a minimal representative.
Write
s_{i}=n_{j}b_{2i}-m_{j}b_{2i+1}
.
Using the relation
g_{i}
we may assume that
(n_{j},m_{j})
is the minimal positive solution to the Diophantine equation
n_{j}b_{2i}-m_{j}b_{2i+1}
.
The
T
-adic order of each term is
s_{j}
, and they all differ. Hence,
x=0
.

3.2.9

??
shows that the sequences
g_{i},h_{i}
from
??
in case (
\aleph
), (
\beth
), or (
\gimel
) are regular.
Indeed, write
A^{\circ}[\underline{X}]
for
A^{\circ}[X_{i},Y_{i}]
or
A^{\circ}[X_{i}^{\pm 1},Y_{i}]
.
Then
g_{i}
is not a zero divisor, as
A^{\circ}[\underline{X}]
is a domain.
Then the element
h_{i}
in
B_{i}=A^{\circ}[\underline{X}]/(g_{i})
is also not a zero divisor, as
B_{i}
is a domain.
In case (
\daleth
), there is only one relation in the presentation which is clearly a regular element.
In conclusion,
A_{i}
is always a complete intersection over
A^{\circ}
.

3.2.10 Computation
.

Let
(A,v),w,\Gamma,\varphi,\mu,\theta
be as in
??
.
We can compute the differentials
\Omega_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}}
as an explicit finitely presented
A_{i}
-module and moreover we show that all the higher cotangent groups vanish, i.e.
\mathbb{L}_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}}\simeq\Omega_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}}[0]
.

(
\aleph
)

Case
\mu\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}}
.
We introduce a dummy variable
Z=X^{-1}
.
Then we can write the representation as

\displaystyle\frac{A^{\circ}[X,Z,Y_{i}]}{(XZ-1,g_{i},h_{i})},\quad g_{i}=Z^{\ell}Y_{i}^{m}-\alpha\beta^{-m},\quad h_{i}=\alpha^{-e}\beta^{d}\theta^{-1}Z^{e}Y_{i}^{d}-\theta^{-1}\varphi.

Write
\mathcal{I}=(XZ-1,g_{i},h_{i})
.
By
??
,
XZ-1,g_{i},h_{i}
is a regular sequence.
So we know that
\mathbb{L}_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}[X,Z,Y_{i}]}\simeq\mathcal{I}/\mathcal{I}^{2}[-1]
, and
\mathcal{I}/\mathcal{I}^{2}
is a free
A_{i}
module with generators
XZ-1,g_{i},h_{i}

[
19
,
Tag 08SJ
]
.

Consider the ring morphisms
A^{\circ}\to A^{\circ}[X,Z,Y_{i}]\to A_{i}
and consider the corresponding LES of cotangent modules

\ldots\to 0\to H_{1}(\mathbb{L}_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}})\to\underbrace{A_{i}\cdot\left<XZ-1,h_{i},j_{i}\right>}_{H_{1}(\mathbb{L}_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}[Z,X,Y_{i}]})}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{d}_{i}}\underbrace{A_{i}\cdot\left<\mathrm{d}X,\mathrm{d}Z,\mathrm{d}Y\right>}_{\Omega_{A^{\circ}[Z,X,Y_{i}]}}\to\Omega_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}}\to 0,

(3.2.c)

with
\mathrm{d}_{i}
represented by the matrix:

\displaystyle\jac(\mathrm{d}_{i})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}Z&X&0\\
0&\ell Z^{\ell-1}Y_{i}^{m}&mZ^{\ell}Y_{i}^{m-1}\\
0&\alpha^{-e}\beta^{d}\theta^{-1}eZ^{e-1}Y_{i}^{d}&\theta^{-1}\alpha^{-e}\beta^{d}dZ^{e}Y_{i}^{d-1}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\det\jac(\mathrm{d}_{i})

\displaystyle=\alpha^{-e}\beta^{d}\theta^{-1}(ld-em)Z^{\ell+e}Y_{i}^{m+d-1}

\displaystyle=Y_{i}^{-1}(\alpha^{-e}\beta^{d}\theta^{-1}Z^{e}Y^{d})(Z^{\ell}Y_{i}^{m})

\displaystyle=(Y_{i})^{-1}(\theta^{-1}\varphi)Z^{\ell}Y_{i}^{m}.

(3.2.d)

In particular
\mathrm{d}_{i}
is injective and thus
H_{1}(\mathbb{L}_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}})=0
and all higher cotangent groups vanish.

(
\beth
)

Case
\mu\not\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}}(\infty)
, and
\Gamma
is not discrete:

Write
\mathcal{I}:=(g_{i},h_{i})
.
Again,
h_{i},g_{i}
is a regular sequence, and
\mathbb{L}_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}[X_{i},Y_{i}]}\simeq\mathcal{I}/\mathcal{I}^{2}[-1]
.
Taking the LES yields

\ldots\to 0\to H_{1}(\mathbb{L}_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}})\to\underbrace{A_{i}\cdot\left<h_{i},g_{i}\right>}_{H_{1}(\mathbb{L}_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}[X_{i},Y_{i}]})}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{d}_{i}}\underbrace{A_{i}\cdot\left<\mathrm{d}X_{i},\ \mathrm{d}Y_{i}\right>}_{\Omega_{A^{\circ}[X_{i},Y_{i}]}}\to\Omega_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}}\to 0,

(3.2.e)

with
\mathrm{d}_{i}
represented by the matrix

\displaystyle\jac(\mathrm{d}_{i})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}Y_{i}&X_{i}\\
\theta^{-1}\alpha&0\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\det\jac(\mathrm{d}_{i})

\displaystyle=-\theta^{-1}\alpha X_{i}

\displaystyle=-\theta^{-1}\varphi.

(3.2.f)

Thus,
\mathrm{d}_{i}
is injective and thus
H_{1}(\mathbb{L}_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}})=0
and all higher cotangent groups vanish.

(
\gimel
)

Case
\mu\not\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}}(\infty)
and
\Gamma
is discrete:

Write
\mathcal{I}:=(g_{i},h_{i})
, which is generated by a regular sequence.
We get almost the same LES as in the previous case:

\ldots\to 0\to H_{1}(\mathbb{L}_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}})\to\underbrace{A_{i}\cdot\left<h_{i},g_{i}\right>}_{H_{1}(\mathbb{L}_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}[X_{i},Y_{i}]})}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{d}_{i}}\underbrace{A_{i}\cdot\left<\mathrm{d}X_{i},\ \mathrm{d}Y_{i}\right>}_{\Omega_{A^{\circ}[X_{i},Y_{i}]}}\to\Omega_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}}\to 0,

(3.2.g)

with
\mathrm{d}_{i}
represented by the matrix

\displaystyle\jac(\mathrm{d}_{i})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}b_{2i+1}X_{i}^{b_{2i+1}-1}Y_{i}^{b_{2i}}&b_{2i}X_{i}^{b_{2i+1}}Y_{i}^{b_{2i}-1}\\
a_{2i+1}X_{i}^{a_{2i+1}-1}Y_{i}^{a_{2i}}&a_{2i}X_{i}^{a_{2i+1}}Y_{i}^{a_{2i}-1}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\det\jac(\mathrm{d}_{i})

\displaystyle=(b_{2i+1}a_{2i}-b_{2i}a_{2i+1})(X_{i}Y_{i})^{-1}(X_{i}^{a_{2i+1}+b_{2i+1}}Y_{i}^{a_{2i}+b_{2i}})

\displaystyle=\left(X_{i}Y_{i}\right)^{-1}\pi\theta^{-1}\varphi.

(3.2.h)

Also,
\mathrm{d}_{i}
is injective and thus
H_{1}(\mathbb{L}_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}})=0
and all higher cotangent groups vanish.

(
\daleth
)

Case
\mu=\infty
:

As
A_{i}
and
A^{\circ}
are domains we know that
\theta^{-1}\alpha X_{i}-\alpha^{-1}\varphi
is not a zero divisor and thus a regular sequence.
The same argument as the previous cases gives that

\ldots\to 0\to 0\to A_{i}\cdot\left<g_{i}\right>\xrightarrow{\mathrm{d}_{i}}A_{i}\cdot\left<\mathrm{d}X_{i}\right>\to\Omega_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}}\to 0,

(3.2.i)

with
\mathrm{d}_{i}
represented by the
1\times 1
matrix

\jac(\mathrm{d}_{i})=\theta^{-1}\alpha.

(3.2.j)

Likewise, we see that
\mathbb{L}_{A_{i}/A}\simeq\Omega_{A_{i}/A}[0]
.

Using
??
, we obtain a few results.

3.2.11 Lemma
.

Continue with the context of
??
.
In all four cases we have that

\lim_{i\in I}w(\det(\jac d_{i}))=\mu-v(\varphi)+(\inf\Gamma_{>0}-\inf(\left<\Gamma,\mu\right>_{>0})).

3.2.12

Note that if
\Gamma
not discrete, then the “fudge terms”
(\inf\Gamma_{>0}-\inf(\left<\Gamma,\mu\right>_{>0}))
vanish.
If
\Gamma
is discrete, then
\inf\Gamma_{>0}
is the valuation of a uniformizer.
Note that if
\varphi,\mu
define a valuation,
v^{\prime}
as in
??
, then
\Gamma_{v^{\prime}}=\left<\Gamma,\mu\right>
.

Proof of
??
.

We again treat the four cases separately.

(
\aleph
)

Case
\mu\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}}:

We will argue that

\displaystyle\lim_{(\beta,\ell)\in I}w(\theta^{-1}\varphi)

\displaystyle=\mu-v(\varphi)

\displaystyle\lim_{(\beta,\ell)\in I}w(Y_{i})

\displaystyle=\inf(\left<\Gamma,\mu\right>_{>0})

\displaystyle\lim_{(\beta,\ell)\in I}w(Z^{\ell}Y_{i}^{m})

\displaystyle=\inf\Gamma_{>0}.

Then the result follows from (
3.2.d
).
The first equality holds because
\mu=w(\varphi)
and
v(\varphi)=v(\theta)
and
\theta
is
w
-stable.

Clearly
w(Y_{i})=w(\beta^{-1}\varphi^{\ell})\in\left<\Gamma,\mu\right>_{>0}
by the bounds on
v(\beta)
.
The condition
j\geq i
translates directly to
w(Y_{j})\leq w(Y_{i})
, so the sequence
(w(Y_{i}))_{i\in I}
is non-increasing.
If
\Gamma
is not discrete then we can get
w(Y_{i})
arbitrarily small by choosing
i=(\beta,\ell)
with
v(\beta)\geq\ell\mu-\epsilon
for
\epsilon>0
.
If
\Gamma
is discrete then
I
has a maximal element
(\pi^{a},\ell)
as remarked in
??
.
Then
w(Y_{(\pi^{a},\ell)})=\ell\mu-av(\pi)=v(\pi)/m
.
In either case we obtain that
\lim_{(\beta,\ell)\in I}w(Y_{i})=\inf(\left<\Gamma,\mu\right>_{>0})
.

For the last equality we note that
w(Z)=0
, so it is sufficient to show that
w(Y_{i}^{m})=\inf\Gamma_{>0}
.
But as
|\left<\Gamma,\mu\right>/\Gamma|=m
we see that
\Gamma_{>0}=m\cdot(\left<\Gamma,\mu\right>_{>0})
and the result follows from the second equality.

(
\beth
)

Case
\mu\not\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}}(\infty)
and
\Gamma
is not discrete:

From (
3.2.f
) we see that
w(\det\jac(d_{i}))
is constant and equal to
\mu-v(\varphi)
.
The fudge terms
(\inf\Gamma_{>0}-\inf(\left<\Gamma,\mu\right>_{>0}))
vanish in this case.

(
\gimel
)

Case
\mu\not\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{Q}}(\infty)
, and
\Gamma
is discrete:

Similarly to case (
\aleph
) we will show that

\displaystyle w(\theta^{-1}\varphi)

\displaystyle=\mu-v(\varphi)

\displaystyle w(\pi)

\displaystyle=\inf\Gamma_{>0}

\displaystyle w(X_{i}Y_{i})

\displaystyle=\inf\left<\Gamma_{>0},\mu\right>_{>0}=0.

Then the result follows from
??
.
The first equality follows from the exact same arguments as the previous case.
By construction
v(\pi)=\inf\Gamma_{>0}
and
\pi
is constant, whence the second equality holds.
Finally, by classical results on continued fractions, we know that

\displaystyle\left|\gamma^{-1}(\mu-v(\varphi))-\frac{a_{i}}{b_{i}}\right|\leq\frac{1}{b_{i}^{2}},\text{ thus }\left|b_{i}(\mu-v(\varphi))-\gamma a_{i}\right|\leq\frac{\gamma}{b_{i}},

from which we learn that
|w(X_{i})|\leq\gamma/b_{2i},|w(Y_{i})|\leq\gamma/b_{2i+1}
.
So both tend to
0
as
i\to\infty
, and we conclude the third inequality.

(
\daleth
)

Case
\mu=\infty
:

We easily see that both sides equal
\infty
.

∎

3.2.13 Lemma
.

Let
A,\varphi,\mu,v,w
be as in
??
,
and let
(L,v_{L})
be a valued field with a map of semi-valued rings
f:(A,w)\to(L,v_{L})
. Then we have

\mathbb{L}_{[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]/A^{\circ}}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}L^{\circ}\simeq\Omega_{[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]/A^{\circ}}\otimes L^{\circ}[0],

and

\cont\left(\Omega_{[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]}\otimes L^{\circ}\right)=\mu-v(\varphi)+(\inf\Gamma_{>0}-\inf(\left<\Gamma,\mu\right>_{>0})).

Proof.

We can write
[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]
as an increasing union of
A^{\circ}
-algebras
A_{i}
as in
??
.
By
??
,
??
,
??
,
??
, the module
\Omega_{A_{i}}
is finitely presented as

0\to A_{i}\cdot\left<\underline{f}\right>\xrightarrow{d_{i}}A_{i}\cdot\left<\underline{X}\right>\to\Omega_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}}\to 0,

for some variables
\underline{X}
and relations
\underline{f}
.
By
[
6
, eq. 1.2.3.4]
, we see that

\mathbb{L}_{[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]/A^{\circ}}=\colim_{i\in I}\left(\mathbb{L}_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{A_{i}}[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]\right).

The functor
-\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]}L^{\circ}
is right exact and thus commutes with colimits.
Then we compute

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]/A^{\circ}}\otimes L^{\circ}

\displaystyle=\colim_{i\in I}\left(\mathbb{L}_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}}\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{A_{i}}L^{\circ}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\colim_{i\in I}\left(\Omega_{A_{i}/A^{\circ}}[0]\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{A_{i}}L^{\circ}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\colim_{i\in I}\left(0\to A_{i}\cdot\left<\underline{f}\right>\xrightarrow{d_{i}}A_{i}\cdot\left<\underline{X}\right>\to 0\right)\otimes^{\mathbf{L}}_{A_{i}}L^{\circ}

\displaystyle\simeq\colim_{i\in I}\left(0\to L^{\circ}\cdot\left<\underline{f}\right>\xrightarrow{d_{i}\otimes L^{\circ}}L^{\circ}\cdot\left<\underline{X}\right>\to 0\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\colim_{i\in I}\left(\Omega_{A_{i}/A}\otimes L^{\circ}[0]\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\Omega_{[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]}\otimes_{[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]}L^{\circ}[0].

This completes the proof of the first part.

The second part, which states that
\cont(\Omega_{[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]/A}\otimes L^{\circ})=\mu-v(\varphi)+(\inf\Gamma_{>0}-\inf(\left<\Gamma,\mu\right>_{>0}))
, would follow from
??
if content commutes with the colimit
{\Omega_{[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]/A^{\circ}}}\otimes_{[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]}L^{\circ}=\colim\Omega_{A_{i}/A}\otimes_{A_{i}}L^{\circ}
, i.e.

\cont\Omega_{[A^{\circ},\varphi,\mu]/A}=\lim_{i\in I}\cont\Omega_{A_{i}/A}=\mu-v(\varphi)+(\inf\Gamma_{>0}-\inf(\left<\Gamma,\mu\right>_{>0})).

(3.2.k)

Suppose that
\mu\neq\infty
, i.e., we are in case (
\aleph
), (
\beth
), (
\gimel
).
It is sufficient to show that the system
(\Omega_{A_{i}/A}\otimes L^{\circ})_{i\in I}
satisfies condition (c) of
??
for (
3.2.k
) to be true, i.e.,

\lim_{i\in I}\lim_{j\geq i}\cont(\coker\Omega_{A_{i}/A}\otimes_{A_{i}}L^{\circ}\to\Omega_{A_{j}/A}\otimes_{A_{j}}L^{\circ})=\lim_{i\in I}\lim_{j\geq i}\cont(\Omega_{A_{j}/A_{i}}\otimes_{A_{j}}L^{\circ})=0.

j\geq i
.
